=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a tree with N vertices numbered 1 to N. The i-th edge connects vertices A_i and B_i.
Consider a tree that can be obtained by removing some (possibly zero) edges and vertices from this graph. Find the minimum number of vertices in such a tree that includes all of K specified vertices V_1,\ldots,V_K.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N K
A_1 B_1
\vdots
A_{N-1} B_{N-1}
V_1 \ldots V_K

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq K \leq N \leq 2\times 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i,B_i \leq N
- 1 \leq V_1 < V_2 < \ldots < V_K \leq N
- The given graph is a tree.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

7 3
1 2
1 3
2 4
2 5
3 6
3 7
1 3 5

Sample Output 1

4

The given tree is shown on the left in the figure below. The tree with the minimum number of vertices that includes all of vertices 1,3,5 is shown on the right.

Sample Input 2

4 4
3 1
1 4
2 1
1 2 3 4

Sample Output 2

4

Sample Input 3

5 1
1 4
2 3
5 2
1 2
1

Sample Output 3

1


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's understand problem: Given tree N up to 2e5, K specified vertices. Consider a tree that can be obtained by removing some edges and vertices from original graph. Find minimum number of vertices in such a tree that includes all K specified vertices. Essentially we need minimal connected subgraph (tree) of original tree containing all specified vertices. Since original is tree, the minimal connected subgraph containing a set S is the union of paths between all pairs in S, i.e., the Steiner tree for terminals in a tree. Its number of vertices equals number of nodes in the minimal subtree spanning S. Need compute size.

Given tree and subset S (V list sorted but no matter). Need count vertices in minimal subtree connecting S. This is common: compress to virtual tree or count edges in minimal subtree = (sum of distances between consecutive terminals in DFS order)/2? For tree, size of minimal subtree edges = (total length of cyclic order tour of terminals)/2. Then vertices = edges + 1 if K>0. Since K>=1. If K=1, edges=0 vertices=1. Need compute distances sum over consecutive terminals in Euler/DFS order, maybe including last to first. Specifically if terminals sorted by DFS discovery order (tin), let d(t_i, t_{i+1}) sum for i=1..K-1 plus d(t_K,t_1). The sum equals 2 * number of edges in minimal subtree (each edge counted twice). Then answer = sum/2 + 1. Need verify.

LCA function:
def lca(u,v):
    if depth[u] < depth[v]: u,v=v,u
    diff = depth[u]-depth[v]
    bit=0
    while diff:
        if diff & 1: u = up[bit][u]
        diff >>= 1; bit += 1
    if u == v: return u
    for k in range(LOG-1, -1, -1):
        if up[k][u] != up[k][v]:
            u = up[k][u]; v = up[k][v]
    return up[0][u]
Could also use for k in range(LOG): if diff>>k &1. Fine.

    it = iter(data)

Let's draft code with readline:

import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    for _ in range(N - 1):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        adj[a].append(b)
        adj[b].append(a)
    V = list(map(int, input().split()))
    # If V line might be empty? no.
    if K == 1:
        print(1)
        return

    parent = [0] * (N + 1)
    depth = [0] * (N + 1)
    tin = [0] * (N + 1)
    parent[1] = 1
    stack = [1]
    timer = 0
    while stack:
        v = stack.pop()
        tin[v] = timer
        timer += 1
        for to in adj[v]:
            if to == parent[v]:
                continue
            parent[to] = v
            depth[to] = depth[v] + 1
            stack.append(to)

    LOG = N.bit_length()
    up = [parent]
    for _ in range(1, LOG):
        prev = up[-1]
        up.append([prev[prev[v]] for v in range(N + 1)])

    def lca(u, v):
        if depth[u] < depth[v]:
            u, v = v, u
        diff = depth[u] - depth[v]
        bit = 0
        while diff:
            if diff & 1:
                u = up[bit][u]
            diff >>= 1
            bit += 1
        if u == v:
            return u
        for k in range(LOG - 1, -1, -1):
            if up[k][u] != up[k][v]:
                u = up[k][u]
                v = up[k][v]
        return up[0][u]

    V.sort(key=tin.__getitem__)
    total = 0
    for i in range(K):
        u = V[i]
        v = V[(i + 1) % K]
        w = lca(u, v)
        total += depth[u] + depth[v] - 2 * depth[w]
    print(total // 2 + 1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Now, maybe there is an issue with the formula when terminals sorted by tin but not by Euler tour first occurrence? Let's prove more rigorously:
Given a tree T and subset S. Let H be minimal subtree. Root at 1. Consider DFS traversal of T that lists vertices when first visited (preorder). The cyclic sequence of S in preorder. For any edge e in H, removing e splits T into two components. The terminals in S are split into two nonempty sets. In the preorder cyclic sequence, the transitions between consecutive terminals (including last to first) cross e exactly twice? Actually if the sequence of S is sorted by preorder, then the terminals in each component form a contiguous block in the preorder? Is that true for any edge? In a rooted tree, the subtree of a node is contiguous in preorder. For an edge parent-child, one component is the child's subtree, which is contiguous in preorder. The other component is the rest, which is also contiguous cyclically (prefix + suffix). Therefore, in the cyclic order of all vertices, the child-subtree terminals appear as a contiguous block. Thus the cyclic order of S has exactly two transitions between the block and the rest. Each transition distance path crosses that edge once? Actually if you go from a terminal in block to a terminal outside, the path crosses the edge; if from outside to block, crosses. So each edge in H is crossed exactly twice in the cyclic tour. Edges not in H are not crossed because all terminals on one side? If edge not in H, removing it, one component has no terminals, so no transition crosses it. Thus sum distances = 2|E(H)|. Good. This relies on preorder contiguity of subtrees. Our iterative DFS preorder has subtree contiguity? Need check with stack pushing all children: For any node v, its descendants are processed before returning to siblings? In our stack, when v is popped, we push all children. The children are above any nodes that were in stack before v (siblings of v or ancestors' siblings). We then pop one child and process its entire subtree, then next child, etc. All descendants of v are processed before any node outside v's subtree? Let's verify: Suppose root has children 2 and 3. When root processed, stack [2,3]. Pop 3, process 3 subtree. Stack initially [2]; when processing 3, push its children on top of 2, so process 3 subtree before 2. Thus subtree of 3 contiguous. Then after 3 subtree done, stack [2]; process 2 subtree. Good. For a node v not root, when v is popped, there may be siblings of v below v in stack? Actually v was pushed by parent along with siblings. LIFO order means v is popped before siblings that were pushed earlier? Let's simulate parent p has children a,b,c. p pushes a,b,c in that order; stack has [a,b,c] with c top. Pop c first, process c subtree. Then b, then a. For c, siblings b,a are below c in stack. When processing c, its children are pushed above b,a, so c subtree processed before b. Thus subtree of c contiguous. For b, after c subtree done, b is top (a below), process b subtree before a. So yes. For a node's descendants, they are pushed above siblings, so contiguous. Good.

Let's write final code with early return and del adj:

```
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    if K == 1:
        print(1)
        return

    adj = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    for _ in range(N - 1):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        adj[a].append(b)
        adj[b].append(a)

    V = list(map(int, input().split()))

    parent = [0] * (N + 1)
    depth = [0] * (N + 1)
    tin = [0] * (N + 1)
    parent[1] = 1
    stack = [1]
    timer = 0
    while stack:
        v = stack.pop()
        tin[v] = timer
        timer += 1
        for to in adj[v]:
            if to == parent[v]:
                continue
            parent[to] = v
            depth[to] = depth[v] + 1
            stack.append(to)

    del adj

    LOG = N.bit_length()
    up = [parent]
    for _ in range(1, LOG):
        prev = up[-1]
        up.append([prev[prev[v]] for v in range(N + 1)])

    def lca(u, v):
        if depth[u] < depth[v]:
            u, v = v, u
        diff = depth[u] - depth[v]
        bit = 0
        while diff:
            if diff & 1:
                u = up[bit][u]
            diff >>= 1
            bit += 1
        if u == v:
            return u
        for k in range(LOG - 1, -1, -1):
            if up[k][u] != up[k][v]:
                u = up[k][u]
                v = up[k][v]
        return up[0][u]

    V.sort(key=tin.__getitem__)
    total = 0
    for i in range(K):
        u = V[i]
        v = V[(i + 1) % K]
        w = lca(u, v)
        total += depth[u] + depth[v] - 2 * depth[w]

    print(total // 2 + 1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Let's maybe optimize lca a bit by using local variable bindings:
```
    def lca(u, v):
        if depth[u] < depth[v]:
            u, v = v, u
        diff = depth[u] - depth[v]
        bit = 0
        while diff:
            if diff & 1:
                u = up[bit][u]
            diff >>= 1
            bit += 1
        if u == v:
            return u
        for k in range(LOG - 1, -1, -1):
            uu = up[k][u]
            vv = up[k][v]
            if uu != vv:
                u = uu
                v = vv
        return up[0][u]
```
This avoids double indexing. Good. Let's use this.

Could also compute distance inline:
```
        w = lca(u, v)
        total += depth[u] + depth[v] - 2 * depth[w]
```

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the code if the tree is rooted at 1 and the parent list is built with `parent[to] = v` but `to` is an int object from adj; if the same node ID appears as an int object multiple times in adj (e.g., root appears in adj of all children), parent list for children stores root int 1 (interned), not the root int object from adj? So there can be multiple int objects for the same node ID in adjacency. Parent list for children of x stores the int object for x that came from stack when processing x. Which int object is that? When x was discovered from its parent, the `to` int object from adj[parent] was appended to stack. So stack holds the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). When processing x, `v` is that int object. It assigns parent[child] = v for each child. So parent list for children of x references the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). Wait parent[child] = v (the int object from parent edge), not the int object from the child edge. The int objects for x from edges to its children are in adj[child] and may be freed after del adj if not referenced elsewhere. That's okay because we only need the value, not identity. Up table stores references to the parent value int object. So all node IDs that appear as ancestors have at least one int object in parent list. Good.

So there can be multiple int objects for the same node ID in adjacency. Parent list for children of x stores the int object for x that came from stack when processing x. Which int object is that? When x was discovered from its parent, the `to` int object from adj[parent] was appended to stack. So stack holds the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). When processing x, `v` is that int object. It assigns parent[child] = v for each child. So parent list for children of x references the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). The int object for x from edges to its children (appended to adj[child]) may not be referenced by parent list unless x is also parent of some node? Wait parent[child] = v (the int object from parent edge), not the int object from the child edge. So for each node x, the int object used as parent value for its children is the one from the edge where x was discovered. The int objects for x from edges to its children are in adj[child] and may be freed after del adj if not referenced elsewhere. That's okay because we only need the value, not identity. Up table stores references to the parent value int object. So all node IDs that appear as ancestors have at least one int object in parent list. Good.
This nuance doesn't affect correctness. Memory after del adj: parent list references one int object per non-leaf node (the discovery edge object). Other duplicate int objects for internal nodes from child edges are freed. Good.

When x was discovered from its parent, the `to` int object from adj[parent] was appended to stack. So stack holds the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). When processing x, `v` is that int object. It assigns parent[child] = v for each child. So parent list for children of x references the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). Wait parent[child] = v (the int object from parent edge), not the int object from the child edge. Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the code if the tree is rooted at 1 and the parent list is built with `parent[to] = v` but `to` is an int object from adj; if the same node ID appears as an int object multiple times in adj (e.g., root appears in adj of all children), parent list for children stores root int 1 (interned), not the root int object from adj? Actually root is 1, small int interned. For other internal nodes, their ID int object appears in adj of their parent and in adj of their children? In adjacency, a node x appears in adj of its parent (as child) and in adj of its children (as parent). The int object for x from input is one object; it is appended to adj[parent] and adj[child]? Wait for edge (x, child), the input line has two int objects: x and child. We append child to adj[x] and x to adj[child]. So the int object for x from that edge is appended to adj[child]. The int object for x from the edge (parent, x) is appended to adj[parent]. These are two different int objects with the same value x, because each input token creates a separate int object. So there can be multiple int objects for the same node ID in adjacency. Parent list for children of x stores the int object for x that came from stack when processing x. Which int object is that? When x was discovered from its parent, the `to` int object from adj[parent] was appended to stack. So stack holds the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). When processing x, `v` is that int object. It assigns parent[child] = v for each child. So parent list for children of x references the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). The int object for x from edges to its children (appended to adj[child]) may not be referenced by parent list unless x is also parent of some node? Wait parent[child] = v (the int object from parent edge), not the int object from the child edge. So for each node x, the int object used as parent value for its children is the one from the edge where x was discovered. The int objects for x from edges to its children are in adj[child] and may be freed after del adj if not referenced elsewhere. That's okay because we only need the value, not identity. Up table stores references to the parent value int object. So all node IDs that appear as ancestors have at least one int object in parent list. Good.

Up table stores references to the parent value int object. So all node IDs that appear as ancestors have at least one int object in parent list. Good.
This nuance doesn't affect correctness. Memory after del adj: parent list references one int object per non-leaf node (the discovery edge object). Other duplicate int objects for internal nodes from child edges are freed. Good.

So there can be multiple int objects for the same node ID in adjacency. Parent list for children of x stores the int object for x that came from stack when processing x. Which int object is that? When x was discovered from its parent, the `to` int object from adj[parent] was appended to stack. So stack holds the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). When processing x, `v` is that int object. It assigns parent[child] = v for each child. So parent list for children of x references the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). Wait parent[child] = v (the int object from parent edge), not the int object from the child edge. Up table stores references to the parent value int object. So all node IDs that appear as ancestors have at least one int object in parent list. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the code if the tree is rooted at 1 and the parent list is built with `parent[to] = v` but `to` is an int object from adj; if the same node ID appears as an int object multiple times in adj (e.g., root appears in adj of all children), parent list for children stores root int 1 (interned), not the root int object from adj? So there can be multiple int objects for the same node ID in adjacency. Parent list for children of x stores the int object for x that came from stack when processing x. Which int object is that? When x was discovered from its parent, the `to` int object from adj[parent] was appended to stack. So stack holds the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). When processing x, `v` is that int object. It assigns parent[child] = v for each child. So parent list for children of x references the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). Wait parent[child] = v (the int object from parent edge), not the int object from the child edge. So for each node x, the int object used as parent value for its children is the one from the edge where x was discovered. The int objects for x from edges to its children are in adj[child] and may be freed after del adj if not referenced elsewhere. That's okay because we only need the value, not identity. Up table stores references to the parent value int object. So all node IDs that appear as ancestors have at least one int object in parent list. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the code if the tree is rooted at 1 and the parent list is built with `parent[to] = v` but `to` is an int object from adj; if the same node ID appears as an int object multiple times in adj (e.g., root appears in adj of all children), parent list for children stores root int 1 (interned), not the root int object from adj? Actually root is 1, small int interned. For other internal nodes, their ID int object appears in adj of their parent and in adj of their children? In adjacency, a node x appears in adj of its parent (as child) and in adj of its children (as parent). The int object for x from input is one object; it is appended to adj[parent] and adj[child]? Wait for edge (x, child), the input line has two int objects: x and child. We append child to adj[x] and x to adj[child]. So the int object for x from that edge is appended to adj[child]. The int object for x from the edge (parent, x) is appended to adj[parent]. These are two different int objects with the same value x, because each input token creates a separate int object. So there can be multiple int objects for the same node ID in adjacency. Parent list for children of x stores the int object for x that came from stack when processing x. Which int object is that? When x was discovered from its parent, the `to` int object from adj[parent] was appended to stack. So stack holds the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). When processing x, `v` is that int object. It assigns parent[child] = v for each child. So parent list for children of x references the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). The int object for x from edges to its children (appended to adj[child]) may not be referenced by parent list unless x is also parent of some node? Wait parent[child] = v (the int object from parent edge), not the int object from the child edge. So for each node x, the int object used as parent value for its children is the one from the edge where x was discovered. The int objects for x from edges to its children are in adj[child] and may be freed after del adj if not referenced elsewhere. That's okay because we only need the value, not identity. Up table stores references to the parent value int object. So all node IDs that appear as ancestors have at least one int object in parent list. Good.

When x was discovered from its parent, the `to` int object from adj[parent] was appended to stack. So stack holds the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). When processing x, `v` is that int object. It assigns parent[child] = v for each child. So parent list for children of x references the int object for x from the edge (parent,x). Wait parent[child] = v (the int object from parent edge), not the int object from the child edge. So for each node x, the int object used as parent value for its children is the one from the edge where x was discovered. The int objects for x from edges to its children are in adj[child] and may be freed after del adj if not referenced elsewhere. That's okay because we only need the value, not identity. Up table stores references to the parent value int object. So all node IDs that appear as ancestors have at least one int object in parent list. Good.
This nuance doesn't affect correctness. Memory after del adj: parent list references one int object per non-leaf node (the discovery edge object). Other duplicate int objects for internal nodes from child edges are freed. Good.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N, K = map(int, input().split())

    if K == 1:
        print(1)
        return

    adj = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    for _ in range(N - 1):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        adj[a].append(b)
        adj[b].append(a)

    V = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))

    parent = [0] * (N + 1)
    depth = [0] * (N + 1)
    tin = [0] * (N + 1)

    parent[1] = 1
    stack = [1]
    timer = 0

    while stack:
        v = stack.pop()
        tin[v] = timer
        timer += 1
        for to in adj[v]:
            if to == parent[v]:
                continue
            parent[to] = v
            depth[to] = depth[v] + 1
            stack.append(to)

    del adj

    LOG = N.bit_length()
    up = [parent]
    for _ in range(1, LOG):
        prev = up[-1]
        up.append([prev[prev[v]] for v in range(N + 1)])

    def lca(u, v):
        if depth[u] < depth[v]:
            u, v = v, u

        diff = depth[u] - depth[v]
        bit = 0
        while diff:
            if diff & 1:
                u = up[bit][u]
            diff >>= 1
            bit += 1

        if u == v:
            return u

        for k in range(LOG - 1, -1, -1):
            nu = up[k][u]
            nv = up[k][v]
            if nu != nv:
                u = nu
                v = nv

        return up[0][u]

    V.sort(key=tin.__getitem__)

    total = 0
    for i in range(K):
        u = V[i]
        v = V[(i + 1) % K]
        w = lca(u, v)
        total += depth[u] + depth[v] - 2 * depth[w]

    print(total // 2 + 1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
